<header>CENTURY V</header>

Barbarian and French in perpetual strife.

->LXXXI.<-

The Royal bird <note>The "Royal bird" typically refers to an eagle, a symbol of imperial power (such as the Holy Roman Empire).</note> over the solar city <note>The "solar city" could refer to Heliopolis, or symbolically to a city like Paris or Rome.</note>,
Seven months before will be a nocturnal omen:
The Eastern wall will fall, thunder and lightning,
Seven days at the gates, the enemies arrive at the appointed hour.

->LXXXII.<-

At the conclusion of the pact outside the fortress,
He who is driven to despair will not come out:
When those of Arbois, of Langres, against Bresse <note>Arbois, Langres, and Bresse are regions and towns in eastern France and the Duchy of Burgundy.</note>
Have the mountains of Dole <note>Dole was then the capital of the Franche-Comté.</note>, an ambush of enemies.

->LXXXIII.<-

Those who have undertaken to subvert
A peerless kingdom, powerful and invincible:
Will cause three nights of warning through deception,
When the greatest one reads the Bible at the table.

->LXXXIV.<-

He will be born of the gulf and the unmeasured city,
Born of obscure and dark parents:
Who will wish to destroy the revered power of the great King,
Through Rouen and Évreux <note>Two major cities in Normandy, France.</note>.

->LXXXV.<-

Through the Suebi <term>Suebi: An ancient Germanic people; Nostradamus uses the term to refer to the inhabitants of Swabia or Germany.</term> and surrounding places,
They will be at war because of the clouds <note>This could refer to atmospheric conditions or metaphorically to "clouds" of religious or political confusion.</note>:
A swarm of marine locusts and gnats <note>original: "couſins," which in this context refers to gnats or mosquitoes.</note>,
The faults of Lake Geneva <term>Leman: From "Lac Léman," the French name for Lake Geneva.</term> will be well laid bare.

->LXXXVI.<-

By the two heads and three separate arms,
The great city will be troubled by waters:

<header>CENTURY V</header>

The Great ones among them scattered by exile,
The head of Persia <note>Persia refers to the Safavid Empire, a frequent subject of concern for 16th-century Europeans due to its wars with the Ottomans.</note>, Byzantium <note>Byzantium refers to Constantinople (Istanbul), the capital of the Ottoman Empire.</note> hard-pressed.

->LXXXVII.<-

The year that Saturn is out of servitude <note>In astrology, a planet is in "servitude" when it is in a sign where its influence is weakened; Nostradamus suggests a shift in celestial power.</note>,

Of Trojan blood <note>Renaissance French royalty often claimed descent from Francus, a mythical survivor of Troy, to rival the Roman myth of Aeneas.</note> will be his marriage,

->LXXXVIII.<-

Upon the sand by a hideous flood,
A sea monster from other seas is found:
Near that place a refuge will be made,
Holding Savona <note>A port city in Liguria, Italy.</note> a slave of Turin.

->LXXXIX.<-

Against Orléans there will be commotions.

->XC.<-

In the Cyclades <note>A group of Greek islands in the Aegean Sea.</note>, in Perinthus and Larissa <note>Ancient cities in Thrace and Thessaly, Greece.</note>,
Within Sparta and all the Peloponnese:
Such great famine, plague by false connivance <note>original: "coninſſe." This is widely considered a printer's error for *connivence* (plotting or complicity).</note>:
It will last nine months, through all the Chersonese <note>The Gallipoli Peninsula.</note>.

---

->XCI.<-

At the great market that is called [the market] of liars,
Of the whole Torrent and Athenian field:
They will be surprised by the light horses,
By Albanians [under] Mars, Leo, [and] Saturn in Aquarius <note>original: "vn verſien." Likely a corruption of "Verseau" (Aquarius), describing a specific astrological alignment of the planets Mars, Leo (the sign), and Saturn.</note>.

After the siege held seventeen years.

# CENTURY V

Five will change in such a revolved term:
Then one will be elected at the same time,
Who will not be very conforming to the Romans <note>This likely refers to a Pope or a leader whose policies or character clash with the established Roman Catholic Church.</note>.

Under the territory of the round lunar globe <note>Astrologically, this refers to the influence of the Moon, which was thought to govern moisture, cycles, and certain geographic regions.</note>,

# CENTURY VI.

One will await a very strange century:
In the year seven hundred and three (the Heavens as witness),
Several kingdoms, from one to five, will undergo change.

->III.<-

The river that tests the new non-Celtic <note>Nostradamus frequently uses "Celtic" to refer to the French; "non-Celtic" here implies a foreigner or someone outside the French lineage.</note> [individual]
Will be in great discord within the empire:
The young Prince, through the ecclesiastical people <note>The clergy or members of the Church.</note>,
Will have the scepter taken, the crown of concord [lost].

->IV.<-

The Celtic river <note>Likely the Rhine, given the mention of Cologne in the next line.</note> will change its bank,
It will no longer hold the city of Agrippina <note>original: "Agripine"; the Latin name for the city of Cologne is *Colonia Claudia Ara Agrippinensium*.</note>:
Everything will be transmuted except for the old language,
Saturn, Leo, Mars, and Cancer in plundering <note>An astrological configuration suggesting a period of intense war or celestial influence.</note>.

->V.<-

So great a famine caused by a pestilential wave,
Through long rain along the Arctic pole:
Samarobryn <note>Often interpreted as a cryptic reference to the city of Amiens, whose ancient name was *Samarobriva*.</note> a hundred leagues from the hemisphere,
They will live without law, exempt from politics.

->VI.<-

There will appear toward the North <note>original: "Septentrion," a classical term for the North derived from the seven stars of the Big Dipper.</note>,
Not far from the sign of Cancer, the hairy star <note>A traditional description of a comet, referring to its luminous tail or "coma."</note>:
Susa, Siena, Boeotia, Eretria,
The great one of Rome will die, the night having vanished.

->VII.<-

Norway and Dacia <note>A region of Central Europe, primarily modern-day Romania.</note> and the British Isle,
Will be vexed by the united brothers:
The Roman leader, sprung from Gallic blood <note>A Pope or leader of the Holy Roman Empire with French ancestry.</note>,
And the troops driven back into the forests.

->VIII.<-

<summary>This page outlines a series of apocalyptic visions for the "sixth century," featuring astrological omens like a comet near Cancer and the shifting of the Rhine river near Cologne. It predicts political instability for a "young Prince" and a "Roman leader" of French descent amidst widespread famine and war in Northern Europe.</summary>
<keywords>Cologne, comet, astrology, famine, Gallic blood, Dacia, Northern Europe, ecclesiastical discord</keywords>

# CENTURY VI.

<margin>witness</margin>Those who were in power through knowledge,
<margin>change</margin>By royal change they will become impoverished:
<margin>ace</margin>Exiles without support, having no gold,
<margin>titles</margin>Titles and letters will no longer be held in high value <note>This suggests a societal shift where intellectual or legal status is stripped away by a new regime.</note>.

<margin>Celtic</margin>->IX.<-
In the holy temples there will be great scandals,
<margin>accounts</margin>Honors and praises will be reckoned
<margin>one</margin>To one whose medals are engraved in silver and gold,
The end will be in very strange torments.

->X.<-
<margin>blood</margin>In a short time, the temples of colors,
Of white and black, the two intermingled:
Red and yellow will snatch <note>original: "embleront," an archaic French term for stealing or carrying away.</note> what is theirs,
<margin>blood, earth</margin>Blood, earth, plague, famine, fire, and maddened water <note>A classic Nostradamian list of apocalyptic disasters; "maddened water" refers to catastrophic flooding.</note>.

<margin>blood</margin>The eldest will be overtaken by death,

->XII.<-

The Royal blood will hold the Vatican <note>original: "Vitican," a common spelling used by Nostradamus for the Vatican in Rome.</note>:
Flemings <note>original: "Flamens," the people of Flanders.</note>, English, Spain with Speyer <note>original: "Aspire," the French name for the city of Speyer in Germany.</note>,

->XIII.<-

A Capitol <note>A reference to the Roman government or a high legislative body.</note> will not want him to reign at all,
He will be unable to maintain his great burden <note>original: "charge," referring to his office or responsibility.</note>.

# CENTURY VI.

When in Seville a monster in the form of an ox shall be seen.

->XX.<-

The holy union <note>A reference to the "Sainte Ligue" or Holy League, a Catholic faction during the French Wars of Religion.</note> will be of short duration,
Some changed, the majority reformed:
In the ships people will endure [hardship],
Then Rome will have a new leopard <note>The "leopard" was historically a heraldic symbol for the English monarchy, though here it may signify a specific invader or usurper.</note>.

->XXI.<-

When those of the arctic pole are united together,
In the East, great terror and fear:
A new one elected, the great [man] sustained trembles,
Rhodes, Byzantium <note>modern-day Istanbul</note> stained with Barbarian <note>A term often used by European writers of this era to refer to the Ottoman forces.</note> blood.

->XXII.<-

Inside the land of the great heavenly <note>original: "Celique," from the Latin *caelicus*.</note> temple,
A nephew in London is murdered through a feigned peace:
The bark <note>A traditional metaphor for the Papacy (the "Barque of St. Peter").</note> then will become schismatic,
Feigned liberty will be the hue and cry.

->XXIII.<-

Coins of the realm will be devalued,
People will be incited against their King:
Peace made anew, holy laws worsened,
Paris <note>original: "R A P I S," an anagram for Paris.</note> was in such a very harsh plight.

->XXIIII.<-

Mars and the scepter will be found joined,
Under the sign of Cancer, a calamitous war.
A short time later a new King will be anointed,
Who for a long time will pacify the earth.

->XXV.<-

Through an opposing Mars, the monarchy will be [disturbed],
Of the great fisherman <note>A reference to the Pope, the successor of St. Peter the fisherman.</note> in ruinous trouble:

# CENTURY VI.

The young [one], black and red, will take over the hierarchy;
The betrayers <note>original: "produiteurs," from the Latin *proditor*, meaning traitors.</note> will go out on a foggy day.

### X X V I.

For four years the seat <note>A reference to the Papal throne or Holy See.</note> will be held fairly well,
[Then] one will succeed him who is libidinous of life:
Ravenna, Pisa, and Verona will provide support,
Out of a desire to raise the Pope’s cross.

### X X V I I.

Within the islands of five rivers to one,
By the crescent of the great Chyren Selin <note>A famous Nostradamian anagram: "Chyren" is widely believed to represent "Henric" (King Henry II of France), and "Selin" refers to the Greek goddess of the moon, Selene, or the crescent moon symbol.</note>:
Through the mists of the air, the fury of one,
Six escaped, hidden [within] bundles of linen.

### X X V I I I.

The great Celtic leader <note>original: "Le grand Celtique," likely referring to a French King or general.</note> will enter into Rome,
Leading a throng of exiles and banished men:
The great pastor <note>The Pope.</note> will put to death every man
Who was united with the Cock <note>A traditional symbol for France (the Gallic Cock).</note> at the Alps.

### X X I X.

The holy widow, hearing the news
Of her branches <note>Metaphor for children or royal descendants.</note> cast into perplexity and trouble:
He who will be led to appease the quarrels,
Through his pursuit of the Shaven ones <note>original: "Razes," a term for tonsured monks or Catholic clergy.</note>, will be satiated.

### X X X.

Through the appearance of holy sanctity,
The seat will be betrayed to the enemies:
On a night when they thought they were sleeping in safety,
Those of Liège will march near Brabant <note>Regions in the Low Countries, modern-day Belgium.</note>.

### X X X I.

The King will find what he desired so much,

# CENTURY VI.

[When] the Prelate <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop.</note> shall be wrongly rebuked,
An answer to the Duke will leave him dissatisfied,
He who in Milan will put many to death.

### XXXII.

Beaten to death with rods through treachery,
Then he will be overcome by his own disorder:
Frivolous advice felt by the great captive,
Born in fury when Berich <note>A name of uncertain identity; possibly a cryptic reference to a specific person or a location.</note> comes to bite.

### XXXIII.

His final hand by the bloody Alus <note>A recurring enigmatic name in the prophecies, often considered an anagram for a historical figure or a "Sulla-like" tyrant.</note>,
Will not be able to protect itself by the sea,
Between two rivers, fearing a military hand,
The black and angry one will make him repent.

### XXXIIII.

The mechanism of flying fire <note>Likely a reference to early artillery, incendiary devices, or "Greek fire."</note>,
Will come to trouble the great besieged leader:
Within there will be such sedition,
That the abandoned ones will act in despair.

### XXXV.

Near Rion <note>Possibly referring to the river Riou or a specific town like Riom.</note>, and close to the white wool,
Aries, Taurus, Cancer, Leo, the Virgin <note>A sequence of zodiac signs (Aries, Taurus, Cancer, Leo, Virgo), suggesting a specific timeframe or astrological alignment.</note>.
Mars, Jupiter, the Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note> will burn the great plain,
Woods and cities, letters hidden by the candle.

### XXXVI.

Neither good nor evil through land battle,
Will reach the borders of Perugia <note>original: "Perouse," a city in central Italy.</note>,
Pisa rebels, Florence sees itself in a bad state,
A King wounded at night on a mule with black housing <note>original: "housse," a decorative or protective cloth covering for a horse or mule, often associated with mourning in this context.</note>.

# CENTURY VI.

The ancient work shall be completed,
Ruin shall fall from the roof upon the great evil:
An innocent person, put to death, will be accused,
The guilty one <note>original: "Nocent," the opposite of innocent.</note> remains hidden in the thicket in the mist.

->XXXVIII.<-

To the profligate enemies of peace,
After having overcome Italy:
The bloody black one, the red one will be committed,
Fire, shedding blood, water colored with blood.

->XXXIX.<-

The child of the kingdom, through paternal capture,
Shall be plundered in order to be delivered:
Near Lake Trasimene <note>A lake in Umbria, Italy, famously the site of a Roman defeat by Hannibal.</note> taken in the azure,
The hostage troop for having drunk too much.

->XL.<-

The Great one of Mainz <note>original: "Magonce."</note> to quench a great thirst,
Shall be deprived of his great dignity:
Those of Cologne will come to lament him so greatly,
That the great troop shall be thrown into the Rhine.

->XLI.<-

The second leader of the kingdom of Denmark <note>original: "Annemarc."</note>,
Through those of Friesland <note>A coastal region in the Netherlands and Germany.</note> and the British Isle,
Shall cause more than a hundred thousand marks <note>A unit of weight for precious metals or a currency.</note> to be spent,
Vainly undertaking a journey into Italy.

->XLII.<-

To Ogmion <note>A recurring figure in the prophecies, often associated with a "Gallic Hercules" or a restorative French monarch.</note> the kingdom shall be left,
By the great Selin <note>A name often used by Nostradamus to refer to the Ottoman Sultan (Sultan Selim) or the Crescent Moon.</note>, who will do no more:
Throughout the Italian lands he will extend his banner,
He will rule through a prudent counterfeit <note>The term "contrefaict" could imply a physical deformity, a disguise, or a strategic imitation.</note>.

<header>CENTURY VI.</header>

### XLIII.

For a long time it will remain uninhabited,
Where the Seine <note>original: "Signe," likely a variant spelling of the river Seine.</note> and Marne flow around to water it,
Tempted by the Thames and warriors <note>original: "martiaux," referring to those of Mars, the god of war.</note>,

### XCIIII.

By night near Nantes, the Lyris <note>Possibly a reference to the Liris river or a Latinized term for the fleur-de-lys, the symbol of the French monarchy.</note> shall appear,
Marine arts shall bring forth the rain:
In the Arabian Gulf, a great fleet <note>original: "classe," from the Latin *classis*.</note> shall sink,
A monster shall be born in Saxony <note>original: "Saxe."</note> from a bear and a sow.

### XCV.

The governor of the kingdom, very learned,
Unwilling to consent to the Royal deed:
The fleet of Melilla <note>original: "Mellile," a Spanish stronghold in North Africa.</note> by a contrary wind,
Shall return him to his most disloyal [enemy].

### XLVI.

A just man shall be sent back into exile,
By pestilence to the borders of Nonseggle <note>A cryptic place name, perhaps a corruption of a Dutch or German border town.</note>,
His response to the "Red" shall lead him astray,
The King withdrawing to the Frog and the Eagle <note>The "Frog" and "Eagle" are heraldic symbols; the frog was an ancient symbol of the early Frankish kings, while the eagle represents the Empire.</note>.

### XLVII.

Between two mountains the two great ones assembled
Shall abandon their secret rivalry <note>original: "simulte," from the Latin *simultas*, meaning a grudge or feigned friendship.</note>,
Brussels and Dole overwhelmed by Langres,
To carry out their plague at Mechelen <note>original: "Malignes," the French name for the city of Mechelen in present-day Belgium.</note>.

### XLVIII.

The holiness, too holy and seductive,
Accompanied by an eloquent tongue:
The old city, and Parma too hasty,

# CENTURY VI
<sig>D i j.</sig>

He will be betrayed, deceived by an interpreter:

The great folded tapestry will not show,
Except only half of the story:
Driven from the kingdom, the harsh one will appear far away:

->L X I I.<-

Too late for both, the flowers <note>Likely a reference to the "fleurs-de-lis," the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note> will be lost,

The lady left alone in the kingdom,

For seven years she will be weeping with grief,

No peace treaty will be held or settled,
All those receiving will act through deception:

The homespun one captured will pass through the gate <note>original: "serre," meaning a narrow passage, a prison, or literally "claws."</note>
His temple opened, two roasted on the plaster.

# CENTURY VI

At the foundation of the new sect:
The bones of the great Roman will be found,
A marble tomb will appear covered,
The earth to shake in April, poorly buried.

->LXVII.<-

To the great Empire will come quite another,
Far from goodness and even further from happiness:
Ruled by one born not far from the pewter <note>original: "peautre," a metal alloy of tin and lead; often used metaphorically to suggest a lowly or impoverished birth, as in a beggar's bowl.</note>,
Kingdoms to collapse, great unhappiness.

->LXVIII.<-

When soldiers in a seditious fury,
Shall make their blades shine at night against their chief:
The enemy of Alba <note>Possibly referring to the Duke of Alba or the ancient city of Alba Longa.</note> by a furious hand,
Then to vex Rome and seduce the principal leaders.

->LXIX.<-

Great pity will be without long delay,
Those who used to give will be forced to take:
Naked, starving with cold and thirst, banding together,
To cross the mountains making a great scandal.

->LXX.<-

A leader of the world the great CHIREN <note>A famous anagram often interpreted by scholars as "Henric," referring to King Henry IV of France.</note> will be
"FURTHER BEYOND" <note>original: "PL V S O V T R E," the French version of the Latin "Plus Ultra," the personal motto of Holy Roman Emperor Charles V.</note> afterwards loved, feared, dreaded:
His fame and praise shall surpass the heavens,
And with the sole title of Victor, highly satisfied.

->LXXI.<-

When they come to give the great King his funeral rites <note>original: "parenter," referring to the performance of ancestral or funerary honors.</note>,
Before he has entirely given up the ghost:
One will soon see him associated
With Eagles, Lions, Crosses, the Crown sold.

# CENTURY VI.

### LXXII.

By a feigned fury of divine inspiration,
The lady of the great fort will be violated:
Judges wishing to condemn such a doctrine,
She is sacrificed as a victim to the ignorant people.

### LXXIII.

In a great city, a monk and an artisan,
Lodged near the gate and by the walls,
Secretly against Modena, speaking in a cellar <note>original: "caue disant," literally "saying cave," implying secret or subterranean meetings.</note>,
Betrayed for acting under the guise of a wedding.

### LXXIIII.

The one driven out will return to rule there,
Her enemies discovered to be conspirators:
More than ever her time will triumph,
Seventy-three <note>original: "Trois & septante."</note> very certain of death.

### LXXV.

The great Pilot <note>A term often used for a high-ranking naval commander or admiral.</note> will be summoned by the King,
To leave the fleet <note>original: "classe," from the Latin "classis," referring to a naval fleet.</note> to reach a higher position:
Seven years later he will be outlawed,
A Barbarian <note>Likely referring to the Barbary Coast or Ottoman forces.</note> army will come to make Venice tremble.

### LXXVI.

The ancient city of Antenor's foundation <note>original: "antenorée forge." According to legend, the Trojan prince Antenor founded the city of Padua.</note>,
No longer able to support the tyrant:
The feigned sleeve <note>original: "manche fainct." This is ambiguous and could refer to a "feigned handle" of a weapon or a "deceptive sleeve" (monastic or clerical garment).</note> in the temple to slit a throat,
The people will drive his followers to death.

### LXXVII.

Through the fraudulent victory of the one who was deceived,
Two fleets become one, the German revolt:
The leader murdered and his son in the tent <note>The text of this quatrain is incomplete in the original transcription.</note>.

# CENTURY VI.

Florence and Imola pursued within the Romagna.

->LXXVIII.<-

To cry victory for the great crescent Selim <note>original: "Selin," likely referring to the Ottoman Sultan Selim; "crescent" refers to the Islamic symbol.</note>,

Pavia <note>original: "Ticcin," from the Latin name for the city, Ticinum.</note>, Milan, and Genoa do not consent to it,
Then by them themselves, a great King <note>original: "Basil," from the Greek "Basileus" meaning King.</note> is proclaimed.

->LXXIX.<-

Near the Ticino <note>A river in northern Italy.</note>, the inhabitants of the Loire,
Garonne and Saône, Seine, Tain, and Gironde <note>A list of major French rivers, signifying the movement of French people or armies.</note>:
Beyond the mountains they will set up a promontory <note>Likely meaning a fortified camp or a vantage point.</note>,
Battle joined, the Po <note>original: "Pau," referring to the River Po in Italy.</note> swollen, submerged by the wave.

->LXXX.<-

The kingdom of Fez <note>A major city in Morocco, representing North African power.</note> will reach those of Europe,
Their city in flames, and the blade will cut:
The great one of Asia with a great troop by land and sea,
Whom those in blue, gray-green <note>original: "pers," a blue-green or bluish-gray color.</note>, and the Cross <note>The symbol of Christian forces.</note> will drive out to death.

->LXXXI.<-

Tears, cries and laments, howls, terror,
Inhuman heart, cruel, black, and chilled:
Lake Geneva <note>original: "Leman."</note>, the Isles, the chief men of Genoa,
Blood to spill, hunger <note>original: "frotaim," likely a variant of "faim" (hunger) or "froment" (wheat/grain).</note>, mercy to none.

->LXXXII.<-

Through the deserts of a free and wild place,
The nephew of the great Pontiff <note>The Pope.</note> will wander:
Struck down at the trial <note>original: "l’esprave" / "l'espreuve."</note> with a heavy branch,
By those who afterwards will occupy the cup <note>original: "cyphe," from the Latin "scyphus," a drinking cup or chalice, often symbolizing a high ecclesiastical office.</note>.

->LXXXIII.<-

He who will have so much honor and favor,
Upon his entry into Belgian Gaul <note>A historical region encompassing parts of the Netherlands, Belgium, and northeastern France.</note>.

# CENTURY VI.

A time after there will be such harshness,
And it will be so warlike against the lily <note>original: "fleur," likely referring to the fleur-de-lis, the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note>.

->LXXXIIII.<-

He whom Claude <note>Likely referring to Claude of Lorraine, Duke of Guise, or a historical figure associated with that name.</note> cannot reign over in Sparta <note>Nostradamus often used "Sparte" (Sparta) as a cryptic reference to a city with strict or military discipline, or potentially a coded name for a French location.</note>,
He will be so much [involved] by seductive ways:
That from short to long, he will have him arraigned <note>original: "araigner," meaning to seize, hook, or bring to justice.</note>,
That his outlook shall be against the King.

->LXXXV.<-

The great city of Tarsus <note>A historical city in Cilicia, modern-day Turkey.</note> by the French <note>original: "Gaulois," literally Gauls, frequently used by the author to mean the French.</note>
Shall be destroyed, all made captives in turbans <note>original: "Turban," signifying Ottoman or Muslim subjects.</note>:
Aid by sea from the great Portuguese [fleet],
On the first day of summer, the feast day of Saint Urban <note>May 25th.</note>.

->LXXXVI.<-

The great Prelate <note>A high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop or archbishop.</note> one day after his dream
Interpreted contrary to its true meaning:
From Gascony <note>A region in southwestern France.</note> a monk shall come upon him,
Who will cause the great Prelate of Sens <note>A city in France that was the seat of a powerful archbishopric.</note> to be elected.

->LXXXVII.<-

The election held in Frankfurt <note>The traditional site for the election of the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>,
Will have no validity, Milan will oppose it:
His closest [ally or relative] will seem so very strong,
That he will drive [the opposition] beyond the Rhine into the marshes.

->LXXXVIII.<-

A great kingdom will remain desolate,
Near the Ebro <note>The longest river in Spain.</note> assemblies will be held.
The Pyrenees mountains will bring it consolation,
When in May the earth will tremble.

->LXXXIX.<-

Between two boats <note>original: "cymbes," from the Latin *cymba*, meaning a small boat or skiff. This often refers to the ancient Persian execution method known as scaphism.</note> with feet and hands tied,

# CENTURY VI.

Ointment made of honey, and sustained by milk:
Wasps and flies [brought to] an angry love <note>original: "i ne amour fachez." This line is famously obscure; it suggests insects attracted to the sweetness causing a nuisance or a "troubled attraction."</note>,
Cup-bearers <note>original: "Poccilateurs," from the Latin *pocillator*, one who serves drinks.</note> to falsify, the Cup <note>original: "Cyphe," from the Latin *cyphus*, a large drinking vessel.</note> tempted.
->LXXXIX.<-

The shameful, stinking, abominable act,
After the deed, there will be happiness:
The great one excused for not being favorable,
That Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea, often used by Nostradamus to represent naval power or the English navy.</note> will not be moved to peace.
->XC.<-

Of the leader of the naval war,
Red one unbridled, severe, a horrible grip,
Captive escaped from the elder in the pack-saddle <note>original: "baste," a type of saddle for carrying loads.</note>,
When a son, an Agrippa <note>A reference to Marcus Vipsanius Agrippa, or more likely a "difficult birth" (born feet-first), which was historically called an "Agrippa" birth.</note>, is born to the great one.
->XCI.<-

Prince of beauty so graceful <note>original: "venuſte," meaning lovely or like Venus.</note>,
Brought to the head, the second deed betrayed:
The city to the sword, reduced to scorched dust,
By too great a murder, the head of the King hated.
->XCII.<-

Greedy Prelate <note>A high-ranking church official.</note>, deceived by ambition,
He will do nothing except what too much overconfidence brings:
His messengers and he himself well-trapped,
Everything in reverse, seeing [himself like] one who would split wood <note>A metaphor for someone engaged in a difficult, manual, or perhaps futile labor after losing their status.</note>.
->XCIII.<-

A King will be angry with the seat-breakers <note>original: "sedifragues," a neologism from the Latin *sedes* (seat/throne) and *fragere* (to break).</note>,
When armor and weapons of war shall be forbidden:
Poisoned sugar hidden by strawberries <note>original: "fragues," a regional term for strawberries.</note>,
Murdered by the waters, dead, saying "I am trapped" <note>original: "ſetre ſerre," meaning to be squeezed, locked, or held in a grip.</note>.
->XCIIII.<-

<header>CENTURY VI.</header>

By a detractor slandered, the younger born <note>original: "puis nay," referring to a cadet or younger son.</note>,
When enormous and martial deeds shall issue forth:
The smallest part doubtful to the eldest,
And soon in the kingdom, partisan deeds will be done.

->X C V I.<-

Great city abandoned to the soldiers,
Never was there a mortal tumult so near:
O what hideous calamity approaches,
Except for one offense, nothing there shall be pardoned.

->C X V I I.<-

At forty-five degrees the sky will burn,
Fire to approach the great new city:
In an instant a great scattered flame will leap up,
When they want to put the Normans <note>The people of Normandy, often used to refer to Northern forces.</note> to the test.

->X C V I I I.<-

Ruined by the Volsci <note>original: "Volſques," an ancient Italic tribe; Nostradamus frequently uses classical names to refer to geographic regions, in this case, likely central Italy.</note> through fears so very terrible,
Their great city stained, a pestilential act:
To pillage Sun and Moon <note>In the symbolic language of the time, these often represented gold and silver, or the spiritual and temporal powers of the Church and State.</note>, and violate their temples,
And the two rivers to redden with flowing blood.

->X C I X.<-

The learned enemy will turn away confused,
The great camp sick, and indeed by ambush:
The Pyrenees mountains and the Punic one <note>original: "Pœnus," a reference to the Carthaginians or Hannibal's ancient route through the mountains.</note> will be refused to him,
Near the river discovering ancient jars <note>original: "oruche," likely a variant of *cruche* (pitcher or jar).</note>.

->C.<-

Daughter of the Aura <note>original: "l’Aure," often interpreted as a reference to the city of Nîmes (historically Nemausus Aura) or a personified wind.</note>, refuge of the unhealthy,
Where the amphitheater <note>Likely referring to the Roman Arena of Nîmes or Arles, which are still standing today.</note> is seen reaching up to the sky:
A prodigy seen, your evil is very near,
You will be captive, and more than four times.

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page serves as the transition between the end of Century VI and the beginning of Century VII. It features the famous Latin "Caution" (a curse directed at critics) which Nostradamus used to protect his work from being misinterpreted or mocked by those he deemed unworthy, followed by the first three quatrains of the seventh Century.</meta>

<header>CENTURY VI.</header>

# LEGAL CAUTION AGAINST INEPT CRITICS.

> *Let those who read these verses judge them maturely:* <note>original Latin: "Qui legent hoſce verſus, naturé cenſunto."</note>
> *Let the profane and ignorant crowd not touch them:* <note>original Latin: "Prophanum vulgus, & inſcium ne attrectato."</note>
> *And let all Astrologers, Fools, and Barbarians keep their distance:* <note>original Latin: "Omneſque Aſtrclogi, Blenni, Barbari procul ſunto." The word "Blenni" refers to simpletons or "blockheads."</note>
> *He who acts otherwise, let him be rightfully cursed.* <note>original Latin: "Qui aliter faxit, is rite ſacer eſto." The phrase "sacer esto" is an ancient Roman legal formula designating someone as an outlaw or a person dedicated to the gods for punishment.</note>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical horizontal border featuring alternating fleur-de-lis and crown motifs.</image-desc>

# PROPHECIES OF
## Mr. Nostradamus.

### CENTURY VII.

->I.<-

The arch <note>original: "L'Arc," which can mean a bow, an archway, or a vault.</note> of the treasure deceived by Achilles,
To the descendants the quadrangular <note>original: "quadrangulaire," likely referring to a square-shaped plot of land or a specific geometric building.</note> is known:
Regarding the Royal deed, the "how" shall be known,
A body seen hanging in the sight of the common people.

->II.<-

Through Mars opened, Arles <note>A city in the south of France near Nostradamus's home.</note> will not provide war.
By night the soldiers will be astonished:
Black and white, at the indigo <note>original: "l'inde," which could mean the color indigo/dark blue, or potentially a reference to India.</note> hidden in the earth,
Under the holy shadow, you will see the traitor and sound the alarm.

->III.<-

After the naval victory of France,
The people of Barcelona, of Salon, and the Phocaeans <note>original: "Barchinons, Salinons, les Phocens." The Phocaeans were the ancient founders of Marseille.</note>:
Golden ivy, the anvil locked inside the bale,
Those of Toulon <note>original: "Ptolon," generally interpreted as a reference to the port of Toulon.</note> will be consenting to the fraud.

<header>CENTURY VII.</header>

->IIII.<-

**The Duke of Langres besieged inside Dole,** <note>Dole was then the capital of the Franche-Comté region.</note>
**Accompanied by those of Autun and Lyon:** <note>original: "Ostun & Lyonnois."</note>
**Geneva and Augsburg join with those of Mirandola** <note>original: "Auspurg ioinct ceux de Mirandole."</note>
**To cross the mountains against the people of Ancona.** <note>original: "Anconnois." This suggests a military campaign crossing the Alps into Italy.</note>

->V.<-

**Wine will be spilled upon the table,**
**The third will not have that which he claimed:**
**Twice descended from the black one of Parma,** <note>original: "noir de Parme." This could refer to a person's name, a heraldic color, or perhaps the "Black Nobility."</note>
**Perugia will do to Pisa what it intended.** <note>original: "Perouse à Pise."</note>

->VI.<-

**Naples, Palermo, and all of Sicily,** <note>original: "Cecile."</note>
**Will be left uninhabited by barbarian hands,** <note>Likely a reference to the frequent raids by Ottoman or Barbary pirates during this era.</note>
**Corsica, Salerno, and the Island of Sardinia,**
**Hunger, plague, war; an end to untried evils.** <note>original: "maux intemptée." The poet suggests a sequence of disasters that have never been seen before.</note>

->VII.<-

**Upon the battle of the great light cavalry,**
**They will cry out: the Great Crescent is confounded!** <note>The "Great Crescent" is a standard symbol for the Ottoman Empire.</note>
**To kill by night in the mountains, in shepherds' clothing,**
**Red abysses within the deep ditch.**

->VIII.<-

**Florence, flee, flee the nearest Roman!** <note>original: "Flora," a poetic name for Florence.</note>
**At Fiesole a conflict will be fought:** <note>original: "Au Fesulan." Fiesole is a town overlooking Florence.</note>
**Blood spilled, the greatest taken by hand,**
**Neither temple nor sex will be spared.** <note>Meaning that neither religious sanctuaries nor women/children will be granted mercy.</note>

->IX.<-

**The Lady, in the absence of her great captain,**
**Will be entreated with love by the Viceroy:**
**A false promise and an unfortunate gift,** <note>original: "Faincte promesse & mal'heureuse estreine." An "estreine" or "étrenne" is traditionally a New Year's gift or a token of favor.</note>

<meta>This page continues Century VII of Nostradamus’s prophecies. It concludes the final lines of Quatrain XV and follows with Quatrains XVI through XXI. <header>CENTURY VII.</header>

**The very great King will make his entry there,**
**The city then free and clear of its enemies.**

->XVI.<-

**Deep entry made by the great Queen,**
**Will make the powerful place inaccessible:**
**The army of the three Lions will be defeated,** <note>The "three Lions" is often interpreted as a reference to the English royal arms.</note>
**Causing within a hideous and terrible event.**

->XVII.<-

**The Prince, rare in pity and clemency,**
**After having granted peace to his people:**
**Through death, he will change great knowledge,**
**The troubled reign replaced by great rest.** <note>original: "trauaillé." In this period, the word suggested a state of being tormented, burdened, or suffering, rather than just "worked."</note>

->XVIII.<-

**The besieged will break their pacts:** <note>original: "paches." A regional variant of "pactes" (pacts or agreements).</note>
**Seven days later they will make a cruel exit:**
**Driven back inside, fire and blood. Seven put to the axe,**
**The captive lady who had brought about the peace.**

->XIX.<-

**The Nicean fort will not be fought,** <note>original: "Nicene." Likely referring to the city of Nice.</note>
**It will be conquered by glittering metal:** <note>original: "rutilant metal." This is a common poetic description for gold, suggesting the fort falls to bribery rather than a physical assault.</note>
**Its deed will be debated for a long time,**
**To the citizens, a strange terror.** <note>original: "espouuental."</note>

->XX.<-

**Ambassadors of the Tuscan tongue,**
**Will cross the Alps and sea in April and May:**
**He of the calf will deliver the harangue,** <note>original: "Celuy de veau." A cryptic reference that could imply a person's name (like Vitellius), a heraldic symbol, or a specific piece of clothing.</note>
**Not coming to erase the Gallic life.**

->XXI.<-

**Through pestilential Volscian enmity,** <note>original: "Volsicque." The Volsci were an ancient people of Italy; however, Nostradamus frequently used this term to refer to the people of the Languedoc region in southern France.</note>

<header>CENTURY VII.</header>

<margin><unclear>difficult,</unclear></margin>
**[The woman] hidden will drive out the tyrant:** <note>The French "dissimulée" is feminine, suggesting a woman acting in secret or a person in disguise.</note>
**At the bridge of Sorgues the deal will be made,** <note>The Sorgue is a river in southeastern France.</note>
**To put to death him and his follower.**

->XXII.<-

**The citizens of Mesopotamia,** <note>Nostradamus often used "Mesopotamia" (Greek for "between rivers") to refer to the French region of Comtat Venaissin, situated between the Rhône and the Durance rivers.</note>
**Will go angrily against the friends of Tarragona:** <note>Tarragona is a port city in Catalonia, Spain.</note>
**Games, rites, banquets, all the people asleep,**
**Vicar at the Rhône, city taken, those of Ausonia.** <note>Ausonia is a poetic Latin name for Italy. The "Vicar" likely refers to a papal representative, as the region was then a papal territory.</note>

->XXIII.<-

<margin><unclear>confidence</unclear></margin>
**The Royal scepter will be forced to take back,**
**What his predecessors had pledged:**
**Since the ring will be misrepresented,** <note>original: "mal entendre." This suggests the significance of a signet ring—a symbol of authority—will be misunderstood or used to deceive.</note>
**When they come to ransack the palace.**

->XXIIII.<-

<margin><unclear>axe</unclear></margin> <note>original: "ache." Likely a fragment of "hache" (axe).</note>
**The buried one will come out of the tomb,**
**Will cause the fort of the bridge to be bound with chains:**
**Poisoned with eggs of a Barbel,** <note>The Barbel is a freshwater fish; its roe was historically believed to be poisonous if eaten during spawning season.</note>
**The Great one of Lorraine by the Marquis du Pont.** <note>The "Marquis du Pont" was a title usually held by the eldest son of the Duke of Lorraine.</note>

->XXV.<-

**Through long war all the army will be exhausted,** <note>original: "exercite." A term derived from the Latin "exercitus," meaning army.</note>
**So that they will find no money for the soldiers:**
**Instead of gold or silver, they will come to coin leather,** <note>A reference to emergency currency used during times of extreme economic distress or sieges.</note>
**Gallic brass, the sign of the crescent Moon.**

->XXVI.<-

**Foists and galleys around seven ships,** <note>A "foist" (fuste) was a small, fast, light-draft galley.</note>
**A mortal war will be joined:**
**The leader of Madrid will receive a blow from arrows,** <note>original: "vires." This refers to a specific type of bolt used in crossbows that spins in flight.</note>
**Two escaped, and five led to shore.**

->XXVII.<-

<summary>This page contains several cryptic prophecies involving political assassinations near Avignon, the use of emergency leather currency during an exhausting war, and a naval skirmish involving Spanish forces. It highlights the transition from traditional wealth to desperation and the vulnerability of leaders to betrayal and poison.</summary>

<keywords>Sorgues, Mesopotamia, Tarragona, Ausonia, economic collapse, leather money, Lorraine, naval warfare, Madrid, Barbel poisoning</keywords>

# CENTURY VII.

**At the fear of Vasto the great cavalry [shall be],** <note>The "Marquis del Vasto" was a famous Spanish-Italian general; the name also suggests "the void" or "vastness."</note>
**Near Ferrara hindered by the baggage:**
**At Turin they will commit such robbery,**
**That from within the fort they will carry off their hostage.**

->XXVIII.<-

**The captain will lead a great prey,**
**Upon the mountain nearest to the enemy:**
**Encircled by fire shall be such a path,**
**All having escaped, except thirty put to the spit.** <note>original: "mis en broche." This likely refers to being impaled on swords or bayonets, or perhaps literal execution.</note>

->XXIX.<-

**The great Duke of Alba will come to rebel,** <note>The Duke of Alba was a prominent Spanish general and advisor to Philip II.</note>
**Against his forefathers he will commit treason:** <note>original: "tradiment" (from the Italian *tradimento*).</note>
**The great [Duke] of Guise will come to defeat him,** <note>original: "debeller," from the Latin *debellare*, meaning to finish a war or utterly conquer. The House of Guise was the primary rival to the Spanish interests in France.</note>
**Led captive and a monument erected.**

->XXX.<-

**The sack approaches, fire and much blood shed,**
**Po, great rivers, the enterprise for the cowherds:** <note>The Po is the longest river in Italy. "Cowherds" (bouviers) may refer to common soldiers or a specific faction of lower-class insurgents.</note>
**From Genoa, Nice, after a long wait,**
**Fossano, Turin, the capture at Savigliano.**

->XXXI.<-

**From Languedoc and Guyenne more than ten** <note>Provinces in Southern and Southwestern France.</note>
**Thousand will want to cross back over the Alps:**
**The great Savoyards shall march against Brindisi,** <note>original: "Allobroges." This was the Roman name for the people of the Savoy region.</note>
**Aquino and Brescia will come to drive them back.** <note>original: "recasser."</note>

->XXXII.<-

**From Montreal shall be born of a cottage,** <note>original: "casane," meaning a small house or hut.</note>
**One who will come to tyrannize over wine-cellars and accounts:**
**To raise an army for the Milanese march,** <note>original: "copie," here used in the Latin sense of *copia*, meaning military forces or abundance.</note>
**Faenza and Florence, exhausting them of gold and men.**

# CENTURY VII.

### XXXIII.

He reigns by fraud, stripping forces away,
The fleet is besieged, spying on the paths:
Two feigned friends <note>original: "faincts amis." This could mean "holy friends" or, more likely in this context, "pretend/false friends."</note> will come to reunite,
Awakening a hatred long since dormant.

### XXXIIII.

The French people will be in great regret,
Light-hearted and vain, they will believe in recklessness:
No bread, salt, nor wine, water, poison, nor ale, <note>original: "ceruoise." An archaic term for barley beer or ale.</note>
A greater captive: hunger, cold, necessity.

### XXXV.

The great pocket <note>original: "grande poche." This likely refers to a person of great wealth or a specific official, or perhaps a nickname for a gluttonous figure.</note> will come to lament and weep
For having elected: they will be deceived in their old age:
Hardly will he want to remain with them,
Deceived he shall be by those of his own tongue.

### XXXVI.

God, the heavens, the whole Divine Word upon the wave,
Carried to Byzantium <note>modern-day Istanbul.</note> by seven red shaven-heads: <note>original: "rouges sept razes." This is widely interpreted as a reference to Cardinals, who wear red and have shaven (tonsured) heads.</note>
Against the anointed ones, three hundred from Trebizond, <note>A historic city on the Black Sea coast of Turkey.</note>
Two laws they will establish: horror, then belief.

### XXXVII.

Ten sent to put the ship's captain to death,
By one who was warned, open war in the fleet:
Confusion at the head, one stings and bites,
Lérins, the Stoechades ships, the cape within the Nerthe. <note>Lérins and the Stoechades (Hyères) are island groups off the French Mediterranean coast; La Nerthe is a coastal mountain range near Marseille.</note>

### X X X V I I I.

The Royal eldest on a vaulting steed,
Will come to spur and gallop so rudely:
Mouth, mouthful, foot groaning in the stirrup, <note>The original French "estrein" is likely a variation of "étrier" (stirrup). The page is cropped here, ending the quatrain prematurely.</note>

<summary>This page contains quatrains 33 through 38 of Century VII, focusing on themes of political deception, famine in France, and naval expeditions involving religious figures traveling toward the East. The text concludes with a fragmented description of a royal figure on horseback.</summary>

# CENTURY VII.

->Dragged, pulled, to die horribly.<-

### XXXIX.

The leader of the French army,
Thinking he will lose the main phalanx <note>original: "phalange." This term refers to the core body of troops.</note>:
Upon a pavement of oats and slate, <note>original: "auaigne & d'ardoiſe." "Auaigne" is an archaic form of "avoine" (oats). This phrase may refer to a specific type of terrain or a logistical route.</note>
He will sink himself through Genoa, a strange people.

### XL.

Inside casks smeared on the outside with oil and grease
Twenty-one will be enclosed before the port:
At the second watch they will perform great feats through death
To win the gates, and be clubbed to death by the watch. <note>original: "guet." The night watchmen or city guards.</note>

### XLI.

The bones of the feet and hands shackled in iron,
Because of a noise, a house long uninhabited,
Will be dug up by those dreaming of hollows, <note>original: "concauant." Likely referring to the act of digging or excavating based on a dream.</note>
A healthy house, now inhabited without noise.

### XLII. <note>The original OCR reads "ZLII," likely a typographical error for XLII (42).</note>

Two newly arrived individuals, seized with poison,
To pour into the kitchen of the great Prince:
Both caught in the act by the scullery boy, <note>original: "ſouillard." The lowest rank of kitchen worker, responsible for cleaning and heavy labor.</note>
He was captured who thought to afflict the elder with death.

# OTHER QUATRAINS
drawn from 12 under the seventh Century:
of which 8 have been rejected because they were
found in preceding Centuries. <note>The seventh "Century" (a set of 100 verses) was famously incomplete in early editions. Printers often filled the gap with these "extra" verses, though many were simply duplicates from earlier books.</note>

->LXXIII.<-

Reinforcement of sieges, spoils of war, and companies of soldiers, <note>original: "manubis & maniples." *Manubis* derives from the Latin *manubiis*, meaning booty or spoils taken from an enemy. *Maniples* refers to the Roman military unit (maniple), suggesting organized infantry.</note>
Change the sacred rite and pass over the sermon, <note>original: "prosne." A *prône* is a specific part of the Catholic Mass where the priest addresses the congregation in their own language rather than Latin.</note>
Captured and beheaded, the triple meadows do not stop them, <note>original: "caputs." Latin for "heads." "Prez triples" (triple meadows) is an enigmatic phrase that may refer to a specific geographic location or a heraldic symbol.</note>
Raised higher from the depths, placed upon the throne.

->LXXX.<-

The West free, the British Isles,
The recognized one passes the low, then the high;
Discontented, sad, the Scottish Rebel corpses, <note>original: "corff. Escotiques." *Corff* is an archaic variant for *corps* (bodies). *Escotiques* relates to Scotland.</note>
Then to rebel further and during a hot night.

->LXXXII.<-

The feigned stratagem will be rare, <note>original: "simulte." Derived from *simuler*, meaning a faked or simulated maneuver.</note>
Death on the path of the rebel through the countryside:
By the return from the Barbary voyage, <note>original: "voyage Barbare." This usually refers to the Barbary Coast of North Africa, infamous at the time for piracy and naval conflict.</note>
They will celebrate the Protestant entry.

->LXXXIII.<-

Hot wind, counsel, tears, timidity,
Attacked at night in bed without weapons:
From great oppression, a calamity, <note>original: "cala mite." Likely a wordplay on *calamité* (calamity). The spacing might also suggest a cryptic reference to a "hushed" or "lowered" state.</note>
The wedding song turned into weeping and tears. <note>original: "epithalame." An *epithalamium* is a song or poem specifically composed to celebrate a marriage.</note>

->THE END.<-

<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the final three Centuries of prophecies to King Henry II of France. It follows the title page for Centuries VIII, IX, and X, which was described on the previous page as being part of the definitive 1568 posthumous edition.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece consisting of two horizontal bands of stylized floral patterns and fleur-de-lis motifs.</image-desc>

# TO THE MOST INVINCIBLE, VERY
## powerful, and Most Christian Henry, King of France the second, Michel Nostradamus, his most humble, and most obedient servant and subject, wishes victory and happiness.

*Because of that sovereign observation that I have had, O Most Christian and most victorious King <note>The title "Most Christian King" (Rex Christianissimus) was a hereditary title held by the Kings of France, signifying their role as defenders of the Church.</note>, since my face, having been for a long time clouded over <note>original: "obnubilée." This suggests a period of obscurity or withdrawal from public life before gaining royal favor.</note>, presented itself before the deity of your immeasurable Majesty, since then I have been perpetually dazzled <note>original: "isblouy" (ébloui). Nostradamus uses the language of "divine right," treating the King as a sun-like or god-like figure whose presence is blindingly bright.</note>, never ceasing to honor and worthily venerate that day when I first presented myself before you, as if before a singular Majesty so humane. Now, seeking some occasion by which I might manifest my good heart and frank courage, so that by means of my power I might have made an ample extension of knowledge toward your most serene Majesty. Now, seeing that to declare it by deeds was not possible for me, joined with my singular desire for my so long darkening <term>obtenebration</term> <note>From the Latin "obtenebrat," meaning to darken or cast a shadow. Nostradamus frequently uses "darkness" to describe his life before royal recognition or the cryptic nature of his work.</note> and obscurity to be suddenly enlightened and transported before the face of the sovereign eye, and the first Monarch of the Universe, so much so that I have been for a long time in doubt as to whom I should come to consecrate these three Centuries of the remainder of my Propheties, completing the thousand <note>original: "miliade." Nostradamus intended for his work to consist of exactly 1000 quatrains (ten "Centuries" of 100 poems each).</note> and*

<summary>In this formal dedication, Nostradamus addresses King Henry II with extreme humility and praise, describing how his life was transformed from obscurity to "dazzling" light upon meeting the monarch. He explains his decision to dedicate the final three Centuries of his prophecies to the King to complete his grand project of one thousand verses.</summary>
<keywords>Henry II, dedication, patronage, royal audience, Most Christian King, Thousand Quatrains, Renaissance rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Centuries VIII, IX, and X to King Henry II. It follows the introductory remarks where Nostradamus describes his initial meeting with the King and his desire to present a worthy work of knowledge.</meta>

after having long meditated with a rash audacity, I have directed my address toward your Majesty, not being for that reason frightened—as the most grave author *Plutarch* relates in the *Life of Lycurgus*—that seeing the offerings and presents that were made by sacrifices in the temples of the immortal *Gods* of that time, and to the end that one should not be too often stunned by the said costs and expenses, they did not dare present themselves at the temples. Notwithstanding this, seeing your Royal Splendor, accompanied by an incomparable humanity, I have directed my address toward you, not as to the *Kings* of *Persia*, to whom it was not permitted at all to go, nor even to approach them. But to a most prudent, to a most wise Prince, I have consecrated my nocturnal and prophetic calculations, composed rather of a natural instinct accompanied by a poetic frenzy <note>original: "fureur poëtique." This refers to the classical concept of *furor poeticus*, a state of divine possession or inspiration that allows a creator to see truths beyond human reason.</note>, than by the rules of poetry; and for the most part composed and harmonized with Astronomical calculation, corresponding to the years, months, and weeks, of the regions, countries, and the majority of the Towns and Cities of all *Europe*, including *Africa*, and a part of *Asia* by the change of regions, which for the most part approach all these climates, and composed of a natural fashion. 

Someone might respond—one who has great need to blow his nose <note>original: "bien besoin de soy moucher." A 16th-century idiom used to describe someone who is foolish, immature, or lacks discernment; literally "needs to wipe his nose like a child."</note>—that the rhythm is as easy as the understanding of the sense is difficult. And for that reason, O most humane *King*, the majority of the prophetic quatrains are so <term>scabrous</term> <note>original: "scabreux." Meaning rough, knotty, or difficult to navigate. Nostradamus admits his verses are intentionally obscure and rhythmically jagged.</note> that one would not know how to find a way through them, nor even less how to interpret any of them. Nevertheless, hoping to leave in writing the years, towns, cities, and re-

<summary>Nostradamus justifies his decision to present these prophecies to the King, explaining that they are based on a mix of "poetic frenzy" and astronomical calculations rather than standard poetic rules. He acknowledges that his verses are intentionally difficult and "scabrous," making them nearly impossible for the common critic to interpret.</summary>

...regions where the majority of events will occur, even from the year 1585 until the year 1606, beginning from the present time, which is the fourteenth of March, 1557, and extending far forward until the event that will occur afterward at the beginning of the seventh millennium <term>seventh millennium: a reference to the "Sabbath of the World," a theological concept where the world ends after 6,000 or 7,000 years</term>, deeply calculated <term>calculated: the original 'supputé' refers to complex chronological reckoning</term>, as far as my astronomical calculation <term>astronomical: in this period, the term included what we now call astrology, specifically the mapping of planetary movements</term> and other knowledge could reach, when the adversaries of Jesus Christ and His Church will begin to multiply more greatly. The whole work has been composed and calculated during well-disposed hours of election <note>In traditional astrology, 'election' is the practice of choosing the most auspicious planetary alignment to begin a specific task.</note>, as accurately as was possible for me. And all of this was done with *free wisdom, and without offense* <note>original Latin: "Minerva libera, & non iniuria." Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom; the phrase implies he worked with an unburdened mind and without intent to cause harm.</note>, calculating nearly as many adventures of the time to come as of the ages past, including the present and what will be known to occur throughout all regions over the course of time—everything exactly as it is written, adding nothing superfluous, even though it is said: 

> *The truth concerning the future is not entirely determined.* <note>original Latin: "Quod de futuris non est determinata omnino veritas." A scholastic principle suggesting that future events involving human free will cannot be known with absolute certainty.</note>

It is quite true, Sire, that due to my natural instinct given to me by my ancestors <note>original: "auites," from the Latin 'avitus,' meaning ancestral or inherited.</note>, without intending to prophesy, and by joining and harmonizing this natural instinct with my long calculations, I have emptied my soul, spirit, and heart of all care, worry, and irritation through rest and tranquility of the mind. The whole was harmonized and predicted, in part from the *bronze tripod* <note>original Latin: "tripode æneo." A reference to the tripod upon which the Oracle of Delphi sat; Nostradamus uses this to signal his method of divine or trance-like inspiration.</note>. Although there are many who attribute to me things that are as much mine as they are nothing at all, God alone, the eternal, who is the searcher of human hearts—pious, just, and merciful—is the true judge. I pray that He may defend me from the...

...slander of the wicked, who would also calumniously inquire for what cause all your most ancient ancestors, the Kings of France, have healed scrofula <term>scrofula: a skin disease known as "the King's Evil," which French and English monarchs were traditionally believed to cure by touch</term>, and how other nations have healed snakebites, while others have possessed a certain instinct for the divining art, along with other cases that would be too long to recount here. Notwithstanding this, for those to whom the malignancy of the evil spirit is not [contained], after my earthly extinction, my writing will be [valued] more than during my lifetime. 

In the meantime, if I should err in my calculation <term>calculation: the original 'supputation' refers to formal chronological reckoning</term> of the ages, it may be according to the will of some. May it please your more than Imperial Majesty to forgive me, as I protest before God and His Saints that I do not intend to put anything in writing in this present epistle that would be against the true Catholic faith, while comparing astronomical calculations according to my knowledge. For the spans of time of our first ancestors who preceded us are such—submitting myself to the correction of the soundest judgment—that the first man, Adam, existed before Noah by about one thousand two hundred forty-two years. I do not compute this time by the reckoning of the Gentiles <term>Gentiles: here referring to non-Christian or pagan historians of antiquity</term>, as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC) was a Roman scholar whose chronological works were often contrasted with Biblical timelines by Renaissance writers.</note> has written, but solely according to the Sacred Scriptures and according to the weakness of my mind in my astronomical calculations. 

After Noah and the universal flood, came Abraham about one thousand eighty years later, who was a supreme astrologer and, according to some, first invented the Chaldean letters <note>original: "lettres Caldaiques." This refers to the writing system of the Chaldeans of Mesopotamia, who were renowned in the ancient world for their mastery of astronomy and magic.</note>. After him came Moses, about five hundred fifteen or sixteen years later, and between...

<meta>This page continues Nostradamus’s "Epistle to Henry II." The previous page concluded with a description of astrological portents and the rise of a Northern power. those horrible temporal Kings <note>Nostradamus often distinguishes between "temporal" (political) and "spiritual" (religious) leaders</note>, such praise shall be given to him by his adherents that he will have shed more human blood of the **innocent churchmen** than anyone could see wine; and this King will commit incredible crimes against the Church. Human blood will flow through the public streets and temples like water from a heavy rain, and the nearest rivers will run red with blood. Through another naval war, the sea shall also turn red, so that the report from one King to another will say: *The sea was reddened by naval wars.* <note>original Latin: "Bellis rubuit naualibus æquor."</note> Then, in the same year and those following, there shall ensue the most horrible pestilence, made more wondrously terrible by a preceding famine. Such great tribulations will occur as have never happened since the first foundation of the **Christian Church**, throughout all the Latin regions, with traces remaining in certain parts of Spain.

At that time, the third **Northern King** <note>original: "Roy Aquilonaire," a term Nostradamus frequently uses to describe a leader from the North, often interpreted by scholars as representing Russia, Scandinavia, or Germany</note>, hearing the cries of the people of his principal title, will raise a great army, and will pass through the straits of his ancestors and great-grandfathers <note>original: "auites & bisayeulx"</note> to restore most things to their proper state. The **Great Vicar of the Cape** <note>A cryptic reference to the Pope, referring to the "cappa" or choral cape worn by the clergy</note> shall be restored to his former status, but he will be desolate and then utterly abandoned. The **Holy of Holies** <note>original Latin: "Sancta Sanctorum"</note> will be destroyed by paganism, and the **Old and New Testaments** will be cast out and burned. After this, the **Antichrist** shall be the infernal prince. For the last time, all the Kingdoms of **Christendom**, and also those of the infidels, will tremble for the space of twenty-five years. There shall be more grievous wars and battles, and towns, cities, castles, and all

Likely referring to the blood of nuns or consecrated virgins</note>, married women and widows violated, nursing children dashed and crushed against the city walls. So many evils will be committed by the means of **Satan**, the infernal prince, that almost the entire world will find itself undone and desolated. After this time has endured for a long while, there shall be nearly renewed another **reign of Saturn** and a **Golden Age** <note>In classical mythology, the Age of Saturn was a lost paradise of peace and abundance</note>. God the Creator, hearing the affliction of his people, will command that **Satan** be placed and bound in the abyss of the bottomless pit <term>barathre: from the Greek 'barathron,' a deep pit or dungeon of punishment</term>, in the deep trench. 

Then, a universal peace shall begin between God and men, and **Satan** shall remain bound for approximately the space of a thousand years. The **Ecclesiastical power** shall return to its greatest strength, and then he [Satan] shall be loosed again. All these figures are justly adapted by the divine scriptures to the visible celestial bodies—namely, by **Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars**, and the others in conjunction—as can be seen more fully through certain **quatrains** <note>original: "quadrins." This refers to the four-line rhymed verses for which Nostradamus is famous</note>. I would have calculated more deeply and coordinated these events with one another, but seeing, O **Most Serene King**, that some of the censors will find difficulty <note>Nostradamus often claimed to self-censor his work to avoid persecution by the religious authorities of the Inquisition</note>, it shall be the cause for me to withdraw my pen to my nightly rest.

Many things also, O King most powerful of all, are illustrious and indeed soon to come <note>original Latin: "Multa etiam ô Rex omnium potentiſſime præclara & ſane in breui vẽtura"</note>, but we cannot and will not include all of them in this your letter; but to understand certain deeds, a few horrific fates must be tasted...

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the eighth "Century" of Nostradamus's prophecies. It follows the conclusion of his lengthy dedication to King Henry II, shifting from the prose letter to the cryptic four-line rhyming verses (quatrains) for which the seer is most famous.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative typographic border composed of two horizontal rows of repeating stylized floral motifs.</image-desc>

# PROPHECIES OF MR. Nostradamus.

## CENTURY VIII.

---

->I.<-

PAU, NAY, OLORON <note>original: "PAV, NAY, LORON." These are three towns in Béarn, but are most famously interpreted by scholars as an anagram for "Napaulon Roy" (King Napoleon)</note> shall be more of fire than of blood,
To swim in praise, the great one to flee to the confluences,
The magpies <note>original: "agessas." In the local Occitan dialect, this refers to magpies, often used to represent gossips or specific petty officials</note> shall be refused entry,
Pampon and the Durance <note>A major river in Southeastern France</note> shall keep them locked in.

->II.<-

Condom and Auch and around Mirande,
I see fire from the Heavens surrounding them:
The Sun <note>original Latin: "Sol"</note> and Mars joined in Leo <note>An astrological timing indicating a period when these two celestial bodies occupy the zodiac sign of the Lion</note>, then Marmande,
Great lightning and hail, the wall falls into the Garonne <note>A major river in Southwest France</note>.

->III.<-

At the strong castle of Vigilanne and Resuiers,
The younger son <term>puisnay</term> <note>A legal term for a younger son, who stands further down the line of inheritance than the firstborn</note> of Nancy <note>The capital of the Duchy of Lorraine; the "younger son" likely refers to a member of the powerful House of Guise</note> shall be confined:
In Turin the first ones shall be burned,
When Lyon shall be overwhelmed with grief.

<summary>This page opens Century VIII of Nostradamus's prophecies, featuring quatrains that blend geographical locations in Southwest France with astrological omens and cryptic references to the House of Lorraine.</summary>
<keywords>Century VIII, Napoleon anagram, astrology, Garonne River, House of Lorraine, Lyon, Turin, Pau, Béarn</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the quatrains of Century VIII, which began on the previous page. The verses move from Southwestern France toward broader European conflicts involving Italy, Switzerland, and the Mediterranean, frequently using the heraldic symbols of the Rooster (France) and the Eagle (the Holy Roman Empire).</meta>

<header>CENTURY VIII.</header>

### IIII.

Inside Monaco <note>original: "Monech." Often interpreted as either Monaco or Munich</note> the Rooster <note>The traditional symbol of France</note> shall be received,
The Cardinal of France shall appear:
By Logarion <note>A cryptic name; scholars suggest it may be an anagram or a corruption of a specific location</note> the Roman shall be deceived,
Weakness for the Eagle <note>The heraldic symbol of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, and strength for the Rooster shall be born.

### V.

A shining, adorned temple shall appear,
The lamp and candle at Borne and Bretueil <note>Towns in Northern France</note>:
For Lucerne <note>The Swiss city and canton</note> the canton turned aside,
When the great Rooster is seen in the coffin <note>A metaphor for the death of a French king or the fall of the state</note>.

### VI.

A flash of light like lightning appears at Lyon,
Shining, it took Malta, suddenly it shall be extinguished,
Sardinia <note>original: "Sardon"</note> and the Moor <note>original: "Mauris"</note> shall treat deceitfully,
Geneva to London <note>original: "Londes"</note> with feigned treason against the Rooster.

### VII.

Vercelli <note>original: "Verseil"</note> and Milan shall give intelligence,
In Ticino <note>original: "Tycin," referring to the region around Pavia, Italy</note> the payment shall be made:
Water, blood, and fire shall run through Florence by the Seine <note>Connecting the French river with the Italian city</note>,
The unique one falling from high to low, making a groan <note>original: "maye." An archaic term for a cry of distress or a trough</note>.

### VIII.

Near Liternum <note>original: "Linterne." An ancient Roman city near Naples</note> inside closed tubs,
Chivasso <note>original: "Chiuuas." A town in the Piedmont region of Italy</note> shall be the intrigue for the Eagle,
The elected one broken <term>cassé</term> <note>meaning deposed or dismissed from office</note> and his people enclosed,
Inside Turin a kidnapped spouse led away.

### IX.

While the Eagle and the Rooster at Savona,
Shall be united, the Sea, the East <term>Levant</term> and Hungary <note>original: "Ongrie"</note>:
The army at Naples, Palermo, the March of Ancona,

<header>CENTURY VIII.</header>

Rome and Venice cry out horribly because of the Beard <note>original: "Barbe." Likely a reference to the Ottoman admiral Hayreddin Barbarossa, whose name means "Red Beard," symbolizing the naval threat to Italy</note>.

->X.<-

A very great stench shall come out of Lausanne,
Whose origin the people shall not know;
All the foreign folk shall be cast out,
Fire seen in the Sky, the foreign people defeated.

->XI.<-

An infinite number of people shall appear at Vicenza <note>An Italian city in the Veneto region</note>,
Without force, fire shall burn the basilica;
Near Lunage <note>likely Lonigo, near Vicenza</note> the great man of Valence <note>Could refer to Valencia in Spain or Valence in France</note> is defeated,
When Venice shall take offense <note>original: "prendra pique," meaning to take a resentment or quarrel</note> through a death.

->XII.<-

He shall appear near Buffalora <note>A town in Lombardy, near Milan</note>,
The tall and noble <note>original: "procere," from the Latin 'procerus'</note> one entered into Milan;
The Abbot of Foix with those of Saint-Maur,
Shall commit a deception <note>original: "forbe," an archaic term for a trick or fraud</note> dressed as peasants <note>original: "vilain," meaning commoners or rustics</note>.

->XIII.<-

The crusader brother <note>Likely a monk or a member of a religious military order</note> through uncontrolled love,
Shall cause Bellerophon <note>A hero of Greek myth</note> to die through Proetus <note>original: "Praytus." In mythology, King Proetus sent Bellerophon away with a letter ordering his execution</note>;
A fleet <note>original: "Classe," from the Latin 'classis'</note> for a thousand years, the maddened woman,
The potion drunk, both shall perish afterward.

->XIIII.<-

Great credit, abundance of gold and silver,
Shall blind honor through lust <note>original: "libide," from the Latin 'libido'</note>;
The offense of adultery shall be made known,
Which shall lead to his great dishonor.

->XV.<-

Toward the North <note>original: "Aquilon," a classical term for the North Wind or the North in general</note> great efforts by a masculine woman <note>original: "hommasse," a woman with masculine traits; often interpreted as a powerful female ruler</note>,
To vex almost all of Europe and the universe,

<header>CENTURY VIII.</header>

<meta>The image shows some ink bleed-through from the reverse side; text on the right edge belongs to the facing page and is ignored.</meta>

Carrying the infection <note>original: "inſect," likely meaning infected or a source of contagion</note> not himself and the pestilence:
Passing the bridge, they will snatch away a thousand miles,
And the bridge shall break at the third resistance.

->XXII.<-

Coursan <note>original: "Gorſan," a town near Narbonne</note>, Narbonne, to be warned by the salt,

->XXIII.<-

Letters found in the Queen's chests,
No signature and without any author's name:
By the statecraft <note>original: "police," here referring to government administration or the management of public affairs</note> the offers will be hidden,
So that it will not be known who the lover <note>original: "l'amateur," implying a secret suitor or a partisan supporter</note> is.

->XXIIII.<-

The lieutenant at the entrance of the door,
Will strike down the great man of Perpignan:
Thinking to save himself at Montpertuis <note>The Pertus Pass in the Pyrenees, a strategic border crossing</note>,
The bastard of Lusignan <note>A famous noble lineage of France and the Crusader states</note> will be deceived.

->XXV.<-

Heart of the lover opened by furtive love,
In the stream he will ravish the Lady:
The half-evil one will feign wantonness <note>original: "laſſiue," which can mean lascivious/lustful or weary</note>,
The father will deprive both of their body and soul.

->XXVI.<-

The bones of Cato <note>original: "De Caton," referring to Cato the Younger, a Roman statesman known for his integrity</note> are found in Barcelona,
Placed, discovered, a site of lands and ruin:
The great one who holds and does not hold will want Pamplona,
Through the abbey of Montserrat <note>A mountain monastery in Catalonia</note>, mist.

->XXVII.<-

# CENTURY VIII.

> ...true <term>axilla</term> <note>original: "auxelle," likely an architectural term or a corruption of the Latin for "armpit," here perhaps referring to a structural joint or corner</note>, one over the other <term>vault</term> <note>original: "fornix," the Latin word for an arch or vaulted ceiling</note>,

->XXVIII.<-

# CENTURY VIII.

### XXXIII.

The great one will be born of Verona and Vicenza,
Who will bear a very unworthy surname,
Who will wish to take vengeance on Venice,
He himself taken by a man of the watch and the signal.

### XXXIIII.

After the victory of the Lion over the Lion,
Upon the mountain of <term>Jura</term> <note>The Jura mountains on the border of France and Switzerland</note> a <term>hecatomb</term> <note>original: "Secatombe," a Greek-derived term for a great public sacrifice or slaughter, literally "one hundred oxen"</note>,

# CENTURY VIII.

The weak band will occupy the land,
Those from the high place will let out horrible cries,
The large herd will be troubled by being cornered,
Near the <term>Ebro</term> <note>original: "D. nebro," a contraction of "d'Ebro," referring to the Ebro river in Spain</note>, the tomb reveals discovered writings.

->LVII.<-

From a simple soldier he will attain an empire,
From the short robe he will attain the long one,
Valiant in arms, in the church or even worse,
To vex the priests as water does a sponge.

->LVIII.<-

A kingdom in quarrel, divided among brothers,
To take up arms and the <term>British</term> <note>original: "Britannique"</note> name,
The <term>Anglican</term> <note>original: "Angelican"</note> title will be considered late,
Surprised by night, led into the <term>French</term> <note>original: "Gallique"</note> air.

->LIX.<-

Twice raised high, twice brought low,
The East as well as the West will weaken,
His adversary, after several battles,
Driven out by sea, will fail in time of need.

->LX.<-

First in <term>Gaul</term> <note>original: "Gaule," the historical name for France</note>, first in <term>Rome</term> <note>original: "Romanie," which could refer to the Romagna region or Rome itself</note>,
By sea and land against the English and Paris,
Marvelous deeds by that great <term>retinue</term> <note>original: "meſnie," an archaic term for a lord's household or military following</note>,
Violent, <unclear>terax</unclear> will lose the <term>NORLARIS</term> <note>This cryptic word is widely considered an anagram for "Lorrains" (the House of Lorraine) or "Le Ronlars"</note>.

->LXI.<-

Never by the light of day,
Will he attain the scepter-bearing sign,
Until all his seats are at rest,
Carrying to the cock <note>The "cock" (Coq) is a traditional symbol of France</note> a gift from the <term>Tagus</term> <note>original: "TAG," likely referring to the Tagus river in Spain and Portugal</note> in misery.
